. Splitting the gauge-field in the background and perturbations (
D.2
), we have:

A_{\mu}=\bar{A}_{\mu}+\delta A_{\mu}=\delta A_{\mu}.

(D.7)

Like scalar perturbations, gauge field perturbations are quantum fluctuations.
In terms of the Stewart-Walker lemma, the quantities which vanish in the FRW universe are gauge invariant
perturbations
[
298
]
.
In this case, it is easy to check that
\delta A_{\mu}
is gauge invariant. As mentioned above, there are only two physically relevant vector modes on the metric side. This implies that the gauge field should have only two physically relevant modes. One of the simplest and most well-known choice is the Coulomb gauge, i. e.,

\delta A_{0}=0,\partial_{i}\delta A^{i}=0\,.

(D.8)

With the above choices for the metric and gauge field, expanding action (
D.5
) to second order in
S_{i}
and
A_{\mu}
, we have:

\mathcal{S}^{(2)}=\frac{M_{\rm Pl}^{2}}{4}\int d^{4}xa^{2}\left(\partial_{j}S^{i}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x\,\left[g_{1}(a,H,M){\delta A_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}-\partial_{j}(g_{2}(a,H,M)\delta A_{i})^{2}\right]\,,

(D.9)

where
g_{1}(a,H,t,M)
and
g_{2}(a,H,t,M)
are functions of scale factor

a
, Hubble parameter
H
. In the case of minimal coupling,
g_{1},g_{2}
become unity.
This is a crucial result, and the EFT we develop in this work depends on this, regarding which we would like to discuss the following points: First,
the vector perturbation
S_{i}
does not have any temporal evolution, while the gauge fields have temporal evolution even if one considers non-minimal couplings of the gauge field with gravity and inflaton field as in
(
D.1
). Since
S_{i}
does not evolve in time, it does not affect the dynamics of the gauge field. Thus,
S_{i}
can be considered an irrelevant degree of freedom. Second,
the above action does not contain any coupling between
\delta A_{i}
and
S_{i}
. Hence, the vector perturbation
S_{i}
does not affect the dynamical evolution of the gauge field
\delta A_{i}
. This is even more interesting considering that the analysis is also valid for non-minimal coupling. Even though initially, the energy density of the gauge field may be negligible compared to the inflaton energy, due to non-minimal coupling, the energy density of the gauge field will evolve non-trivially during
the inflationary phase.
Consequently, non-minimal coupling terms may cause isocurvature density perturbations
[
299
]
, and
the gauge field can not be treated as a spectator field. However, from Eq. (
D.9
), we see that the vector modes do not evolve in time, and the only relevant degree of freedom is the gauge field. See Ref.
[
300
]
for a similar study in the context of inflationary paradigm with a spectator field with non-minimal coupling. Third, the above analysis can be extended to the case where the 3-space is not flat. In that case, also
S_{i}
does not evolve in time. Lastly, we can extend the computation for
f(R)
theories as in second order in
perturbation
S_{i}
will not have any time evolution for this kind of theory.
This conclusion can also be drawn for
Horava-Lifshitz gravity
[
301
]
.

Lastly, constructing EFT magnetogenesis requires consistently counting the number of degrees of freedom to write down the EFT Lagrangian. Hence, we need to find any possible interaction between the vector modes of perturbation
S_{i}
and
\delta A_{i}
. From (
D.5
), one can see that the lowest order
interaction between
S_{i}
and
\delta A_{i}
would come from the standard electromagnetic term

F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}
and is:

F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=2S^{i}g^{jk}\,\partial_{0}\delta A_{j}\,\left(\partial_{i}\delta A_{k}-\partial_{k}\delta A_{j}\right)\,.

(D.10)

As can be seen, this interaction term is third-order. Similarly, the non-minimal terms in Eq. (
D.1
) can also produce higher order interaction between gauge field
and vector modes.
While these are important for higher-order computations where one also has to include scalar and tensor perturbations
[
302
]
; for the current work, these terms are irrelevant. Hence, the interaction between
S_{i}
and
\delta A_{i}
can be ignored for the EFT of magnetogenesis containing quadratic terms, and the only relevant degrees of freedom will be the gauge field
A_{\mu}
.

D.2
Correspondence between EFT parameters and magnetogenesis models

In this appendix, we provide detailed calculations that show a one-to-one mapping between the magnetogenesis model and EFT parameters defined in Eq. (
5.6
). Specifically, we consider
Ratra model
[
98
]
, gravitational coupling of the electromagnetic field
[
97
,
120
]
, higher-order gravitational coupling
[
214
]
and Galileon vector fields
[
102
]
.

D.2.1
Class of Ratra Model with integer exponent

The Lagrangian corresponding to the conformal invariance breaking term in the Ratra model is
[
98
]
:

\mathcal{S}_{\rm Ratra}=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,f^{2}(\phi)F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}

(D.11)

where
f(\phi)
is an arbitrary function of the inflaton field.
While there have been many simple choices, here, we consider one choice
[
303
]
:

f(\phi)=f_{0}\exp{\left(-\frac{C\phi^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\right)},

(D.12)

where
f_{0},C
are positive constants. Note that in the limit of
\Lambda\to\infty
,
f(\phi)\to f_{0}
and is consistent with the series expansion (
5.6
).

For the inflationary potential
[
304
]

V(\phi)=-\frac{8}{3}M^{2}_{\rm Pl}\lambda_{2}\phi^{2}+\lambda_{2}\phi^{4}\,,

(D.13)

the scalar field
\phi
evolves as:

\phi^{2}=\sqrt{3}M_{\rm Pl}H

(D.14)

Substituting the above expression in Eq. (
D.12
), we have:

f(\phi)=f_{0}\exp{\left(-\sqrt{3}\tilde{C}\frac{H}{\Lambda}\right)}\qquad\tilde{C}=C\frac{M_{\rm Pl}}{\Lambda}

(D.15)

Expanding the above form of
f(\phi)
implies that the series will only consist of integer powers of
H
and can be easily mapped to the EFT action (
5.5
).
Recently, in Ref.
[
213
]
, authors have considered magnetogenesis in the Higgs-Starobinsky model. This leads to a generalized form of
f(\phi)
in the Einstein frame. By repeating the
same procedure, we can obtain a one-to-one mapping with the EFT parameters
s_{n},d_{n}
.

D.2.2
Gravitational Coupling

The Lagrangian corresponding to the non-minimal coupling of the electromagnetic field tensor with curvature terms are
[
97
]
:

\mathcal{S}_{\rm NC}=\frac{1}{4}\int d^{4}x\left(-\Gamma_{1}RF_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\Gamma_{2}R_{\mu\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}F^{\mu\alpha}F^{\nu\beta}-\Gamma_{3}R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}F^{\mu\nu}F^{\alpha\beta}\right)

(D.16)

where
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
and
\Gamma_{3}
are coupling constants. For the FRW background (
D.1
), the Ricci scalar (
R
) is:

R=-6\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}^{2}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}{a^{2}}\right).

(D.17)

Substituting in the term in Eq. (
D.16
), we can see that the conformal invariance breaking terms correspond to

s_{2}=b_{1}=d_{2}=e_{1}=-3
in Eq. (
5.6
). Let us now focus on the
second term (
R_{\mu\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}F^{\mu\alpha}F^{\nu\beta}
). For the FRW background, we have:

R_{\mu\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}F^{\mu\alpha}F^{\nu\beta}=-\frac{1}{a^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)(A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}+\frac{1}{a^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}{2}+\mathcal{H}^{2}\right)(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}

(D.18)

Comparing the above expression with Eq. (
5.6
), we see that the conformal invariance breaking terms correspond to
s_{2}=-\frac{1}{2},b_{1}=-1

and
d_{2}=-1,e_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}
. Finally, expanding
R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}F^{\mu\nu}F^{\alpha\beta}
term for the FRW background, we have:

R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}F^{\mu\nu}F^{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}{a^{2}}(A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}+\frac{\mathcal{H}^{2}}{a^{2}}(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}.

(D.19)

Here again, choosing
b_{1}=d_{1}=-1
in (
5.6
) corresponds to the Riemann coupling terms in the above action
(
D.16
).

D.2.3
Higher Order Gravitational Coupling

The Lagrangian corresponding to the non-minimal coupling of the electromagnetic field tensor with higher-order Ricci scalar is
[
214
]
:

\mathcal{S}_{\rm Higher}=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,R^{3}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}

(D.20)

Substituting the Ricci scalar (
D.17
) in the above expression, it can be shown that the above
action has a one-to-one correspondence with EFT action (
5.6
), for the following choice of parameters:

s_{6}=-6,b_{3}=-6,g_{41}=-18,g_{22}=-18,d_{6}=-6,e_{3}=-6,h_{41}=-18,h_{22}=-18\,.

D.2.4
Vector Galileon Model

Let us now consider the conformal invariance breaking term in the vector Galileon model
[
102
]
:

\mathcal{S}_{\rm VG}=2\,D\int d^{4}x\left[-\frac{a^{\prime 2}}{N^{3}a}(A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}+\left(\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{Na^{2}}-\frac{a^{\prime}N^{\prime}}{N^{2}a^{2}}\right)(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\right]

(D.21)

where for the FRW metric (
D.1
) the lapse function
N=a(\eta)
. Comparing
(
D.21
) and (
5.6
),
we see that all parameters except
s_{2}
and
e_{1}
vanish in (
5.6
). We thus get:

s_{2}=e_{1},~{\rm and}~D=-\frac{s_{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\,.

(D.22)

D.3
EFT action with different coupling functions for the components of the gauge field

In general, we can have different coefficients in front of components of the gauge field
(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
in the EFT action (
5.5
). However, as shown in this appendix, this will lead to different propagation speeds for these three components. To see this, we consider the following action:

\mathcal{S}=\int d^{4}x\left\{\left(f_{1}\,A_{1}^{\prime 2}+f_{1,2}\,A_{2}^{\prime 2}+f_{1,3}\,A_{3}^{\prime 2}\right)-\left(f_{2}\,(\partial_{j}A_{1})^{2}+f_{2,2}\,(\partial_{j}A_{2})^{2}+f_{2,3}\,(\partial_{j}A_{3})^{2}\right)\right\}.

(D.23)

First, we can introduce the following field redefinitions of the gauge field components
(A_{2},A_{3})
:

\tilde{A}_{2}^{\prime}=\left(\frac{f_{1,2}}{f_{1}}\right)^{1/2}A_{2}^{\prime};\quad\tilde{A}_{3}^{\prime}=\left(\frac{f_{1,3}}{f_{1}}\right)^{1/2}A_{3}^{\prime}\,.

(D.24)

Thus, (
D.23
) becomes:

\mathcal{S}=\int d^{4}x\left\{f_{1}\left(A_{1}^{\prime 2}+\tilde{A}_{2}^{\prime 2}+\tilde{A}_{3}^{\prime 2}\right)-\left(f_{2}\,(\partial_{j}A_{1})^{2}+f_{2,2}\,(\partial_{j}A_{2})^{2}+f_{2,3}\,(\partial_{j}A_{3})^{2}\right)\right\}.

(D.25)

Here we can notice that the first three terms have the same coupling functions as
f_{1}
, but the action is written in terms of
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
and
\tilde{A}_{2}
and
\tilde{A}_{3}
. To write down the action in terms of
A_{1}
,
\tilde{A}_{2}
and
\tilde{A}_{3}
we next focus on the single component
A_{2}
in (
D.25
) which can be written in terms of
\tilde{A}_{2}
as,

A_{2}=\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{1,2}}\right)^{1/2}\tilde{A}_{2}-\int d\eta\,\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{1,2}}\right)^{1/2}\tilde{A}_{2}(\eta)

(D.26)

then
f_{2,2}\,(\partial_{j}A_{2})^{2}
term in (
D.25
) becomes:

\displaystyle f_{2,2}\,(\partial_{j}A_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=f_{2,2}\,\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{1,2}}\right)(\partial_{j}\tilde{A}_{2})^{2}+f_{2,2}\int d\eta\,d\eta^{\prime}\,\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{1,2}}\right)^{1/2}\partial_{j}\tilde{A}_{2}(\eta)\frac{d}{d\eta^{\prime}}\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{1,2}}\right)^{1/2}\partial_{j}\tilde{A}_{2}(\eta^{\prime})

\displaystyle{}-2f_{2,2}\,\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{1,2}}\right)^{1/2}\partial_{j}\tilde{A}_{2}\int d\eta\,\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{1,2}}\right)^{1/2}\partial_{j}\tilde{A}_{2}(\eta)

(D.27)

\displaystyle=F_{2,2}(\partial_{j}\tilde{A}_{2})^{2}

(D.28)

where,
F_{2,2}
is a function of
f_{1}
,
f_{1,2}
,
f_{2,2}
and
\eta
. A similar exercise can be done for the
A_{3}
component to express it in terms of
\tilde{A}_{3}
, and we can introduce a new coupling function as
F_{2,3}
. Hence, after the field redefinition (
D.25
) can be written as,

\mathcal{S}=\int d^{4}x\left\{f_{1}\left(A_{1}^{\prime 2}+A_{2}^{\prime 2}+A_{3}^{\prime 2}\right)-\left(f_{2}\,(\partial_{j}A_{1})^{2}+F_{2,2}\,(\partial_{j}A_{2})^{2}+F_{2,3}\,(\partial_{j}A_{3})^{2}\right)\right\}.

(D.29)

Here we have omitted the "tilde" from the fields. We can also notice that even if the temporal derivatives of the field components have the same coupling function, the spatial derivative part of the field components have different coupling functions. Hence, the propagation speeds of the three components are:

c_{A_{1}}=\left(\frac{f_{2}}{f_{1}}\right)^{1/2};\quad\tilde{c}_{A_{2}}=\frac{F_{2,2}}{f_{1}};\quad\tilde{c}_{A_{3}}=\frac{F_{2,3}}{f_{1}}

(D.30)

Hence, the propagation speed of the different components is different. The analysis in this work can be extended for this case.

D.4
Energy density for EFT action in terms of Lapse function

Vector perturbations can affect the background metric and matter configuration in which the perturbations propagate. This has been extensively studied for density perturbations during inflation and is also operational for other perturbations like vector perturbations. In order to quantify the effect of these EFT perturbations on the FRW background, we need to obtain the energy density (
\rho_{\rm EFT}
) corresponding to the EFT action (
5.5
).

To obtain the expression for the energy density, it is convenient to use the ADM formalism which simplifies the calculations significantly. The metric in ADM formalism is given by
[
40
]
:

\displaystyle ds^{2}=N^{2}d\eta^{2}-\gamma_{ij}(N^{i}d\eta+dx^{i})(N^{j}d\eta+dx^{j})

(D.31)

where
N
is lapse function,
N^{i}
is shift vector and
\gamma_{ij}
is the metric on constant
\eta-
hypersurface. Note that with
N^{i}=0
and
\gamma_{ij}=a^{2}(\eta)\delta_{ij}
, we obtain flat FRW metric (
D.1
).
In the flat
FRW background (
D.1
), for arbitrary
N(\eta)
, the standard electromagnetic action becomes

\displaystyle S_{EM}=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x\,\left[\frac{a}{N}{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}-\frac{N}{a}(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\right]\,,

(D.32)

and the vector Galileon action (ignoring the total derivative term) is
[
102
]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{VEC}=2D\,\int\,d^{4}x\Big[-\,\frac{a^{\prime}{}^{2}}{N^{3}\,a}\,A_{i}^{\prime}{}^{2}+\frac{2{a^{\prime}}^{2}}{N\,a^{3}}\,\left(\partial_{i}A_{j}\right)^{2}-\frac{2a^{\prime}}{N\,a^{2}}\,\partial_{i}A_{j}\,\partial_{i}A_{j}^{\prime}\Big]\,.

(D.33)

The lapse function does not have a kinetic term, and the variation of the total (gravity and matter) action w.r.t
N
will lead to a constraint. Rewriting the total action
in the form

S_{\rm Grav-Mat}=S_{\rm EH}+S_{\rm tot}=\int d^{3}xa^{3}\int dt\left[p_{a}\dot{a}+p_{A_{i}}\dot{A}_{i}-N\,H_{A_{i}}\right]\,,

(D.34)

where
p_{a}
is the canonical momentum corresponding to
a
,
p_{A_{i}}
is the canonical momentum corresponding to
A_{i}
and
H_{A_{i}}
is the Hamiltonian corresponding to the EM field, including the Vector Galileon term.

We can now extend the above analysis for the EFT action (
5.5
). In other words, the EFT action can be written as:

\mathcal{S}_{\rm EFT}=\int d^{3}xa^{3}\int dt\left[p_{A_{i}}\dot{A}_{i}-N\,H_{\rm EFT}\right]\,,

(D.35)

where
H_{\rm EFT}=\int d^{3}xa^{3}\rho_{\rm EFT}
is the Hamiltonian
corresponding to the EM field with EFT expansion. The corresponding energy density is:

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm EFT}=T^{0}_{0}=g^{00}T_{00}=-\frac{1}{a^{3}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{S}_{\rm EFT}}{\delta N}\,.

(D.36)

Note that in order to use the above method,
it is convenient to rewrite the expansion parameter in terms of the lapse function in the FRW line element (
D.1
).
However, when we wrote the expansion parameters in Eq. (
5.6
) we have set
N(\eta)=a(\eta)
. Hence, we first need to reinstate the lapse function
N(\eta)
in Eq. (
5.6
). This can be done in two steps:

Step 1:

We first write the EFT parameters
f_{i}(\mathcal{H},a,\eta)
, defined in Eq. (
5.6
) (for
i=1,2
), in terms of the cosmic time:

\displaystyle f_{i}(\mathcal{H},a,\eta)\xrightarrow{a\,d\eta\,\rightarrow\,dt}f_{i}(H,a,t)

(D.37)

Step 2:

Rewrite the EFT parameters in cosmic time
f_{i}(H,a,t)
in terms of conformal time and arbitrary lapse function
N(\eta)
:

\displaystyle f_{i}(H,a,t)\xrightarrow{dt\,\rightarrow\,Nd\eta}f_{i}(\mathcal{H},a,N,\eta)

(D.38)

We need to be careful to obtain
f_{i}(\mathcal{H},a,N,\eta)
, as the zeroth order terms (i.e.,
s_{0},d_{0}
) in Eq. (
5.6
) are independent of
N(\eta)
.
We fix these zeroth-order terms in the EFT expansion parameters with respect to the standard electromagnetic action for the metric (
D.1
):

\displaystyle S_{\rm EM}=-\frac{1}{4}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=\int d^{4}x\left[\frac{a(\eta)}{2N(\eta)}(A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}-\frac{N(\eta)}{2a(\eta)}(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\right]

(D.39)

Thus, by fixing the zeroth-order EFT parameters with
S_{\rm EM}
as
a(\eta)s_{0}/2N(\eta)
and
N(\eta)d_{0}/2a(\eta)
, we will obtain the expansion parameters
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. Note that in the setting
N(\eta)=a(\eta)
we get the zeroth order terms as
s_{0},d_{0}
(cf. Eq.(
5.7
)), which are time-independent and can be set to unity.

Following these two steps, the expansion parameters (
f_{1},f_{2}
), in terms of lapse function, take the following forms:

\begin{split}&f_{1}(\mathcal{H},a,N,\eta)=\frac{a}{N}s_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{s_{n}}{N^{n}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{n}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{b_{n}}{a}\frac{1}{N^{n}}\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda^{2}}\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a}{N}\mathcal{H}\right)\right)^{n}+...\\
&f_{2}(\mathcal{H},a,N,\eta)=\frac{N}{a}d_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{n}}{N^{n}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{n}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{e_{n}}{a}\frac{1}{N^{n}}\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda^{2}}\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a}{N}\mathcal{H}\right)\right)^{n}+...\end{split}

(D.40)

As mentioned above, we have separated the zeroth-order terms in the expansion parameters and are fixed with respect to the standard electrodynamics action.
Setting
N(\eta)=a(\eta)
in the above expression matches with Eq.(
5.6
).
Using the expansion parameters (
D.40
) in terms of lapse function and varying the action (
5.6
) with respect to the lapse function
N\rightarrow N+\delta N
gives:

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{S}_{\rm EFT}

\displaystyle=-\int d^{4}x\,\left[\left(\frac{a}{N^{2}}s_{0}+\sum\frac{n}{N^{n+1}}s_{n}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{n}+\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n+1}\Lambda^{2n}}b_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n}\right)\delta N\,(A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.-(A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n}\Lambda^{2n}}b_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n-1}\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N^{2}}\delta N\right)+.....\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.+\left(\frac{1}{a}d_{0}-\sum\frac{n}{N^{n+1}}d_{n}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{n}-\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n+1}\Lambda^{2n}}e_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n}\right)\delta N\,(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.+(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n}\Lambda^{2n}}e_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n-1}\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N^{2}}\delta N\right)+.....\right].

(D.41)

Doing integration by parts in the second and fourth line in the equation (
D.4
)
and ignoring the total derivative terms gives:

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{S}_{\rm EFT}

\displaystyle=-\int d^{4}x\,\left[\left(\frac{a}{N^{2}}s_{0}+\sum\frac{n}{N^{n+1}}s_{n}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{n}+\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n+1}\Lambda^{2n}}b_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n}\right)\,(A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.+\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N^{2}}\frac{d}{d\eta}\left((A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n}\Lambda^{2n}}b_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n-1}\right)+.....\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.+\left(\frac{1}{a}d_{0}-\sum\frac{n}{N^{n+1}}d_{n}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{n}-\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n+1}\Lambda^{2n}}e_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n}\right)\,(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.-\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N^{2}}\frac{d}{d\eta}\left((\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n}\Lambda^{2n}}e_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n-1}\right)+.....\right]\delta N.

(D.42)

Now using Eq. (
D.36
) and Eq. (
D.4
), the energy density corresponding to the EFT action is:

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a^{4}}\left[\left(\frac{a^{2}}{N^{2}}s_{0}+\sum\frac{na}{N^{n+1}}s_{n}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{n}+\sum\frac{n}{N^{n+1}\Lambda^{2n}}b_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n}\right)\,(A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.+\frac{a^{2}\mathcal{H}}{N^{2}}\frac{d}{d\eta}\left((A_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n}\Lambda^{2n}}b_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n-1}\right)+.....\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.+\left(d_{0}-\sum\frac{na}{N^{n+1}}d_{n}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{n}-\sum\frac{n}{N^{n+1}\Lambda^{2n}}e_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n}\right)\,(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\right.

\displaystyle{}\left.-\frac{a^{2}\mathcal{H}}{N^{2}}\frac{d}{d\eta}\left((\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}\sum\frac{n}{aN^{n}\Lambda^{2n}}e_{n}\left(\frac{d}{d\eta}\left(\frac{a\mathcal{H}}{N}\right)\right)^{n-1}\right)+.....\right].

(D.43)

Setting
N(\eta)=a(\eta)
in the above expression and using the truncated series expansion (
5.7
), we obtain the energy density for the EFT action:

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm EFT}=\rho_{\rm E}+\rho_{\rm B}+\rho_{\rm mixing}

(D.44)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm E}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a^{4}}\left(s_{0}+\frac{s_{1}}{a}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}+\frac{1}{a^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{2}\left[2s_{2}-b_{1}(1+\epsilon_{1})\right]\right){A_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}

(D.45a)

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a^{4}}\left(d_{0}-\frac{d_{1}}{a}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}-\frac{1}{a^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda}\right)^{2}\left[2d_{2}-e_{1}(1+\epsilon_{1})\right]\right)(\partial_{i}A_{j})^{2}

(D.45b)

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm mixing}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{a^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{H}}{\Lambda^{2}}\left(b_{1}A_{i}^{\prime}A_{i}^{\prime\prime}+e_{1}\partial_{i}A_{j}\,\partial_{i}A_{j}^{\prime}\right)

(D.45c)

where in the last expression
A_{i}^{\prime\prime}
can be substituted by using the equations of motion (
5.12
) in the real space.

D.5
EFT magnetogenesis power-spectrum for power-law inflation

In Sec. (
5.2
), we obtained the power-spectrum during slow-roll inflation. In this Appendix, we obtain the power spectrum for a generic power-law and de Sitter inflation.

The procedure we follow to obtain the magnetic power-spectrum is the same as in the case of slow-roll. In the case of de Sitter, the power spectrum is exact while in the case of power-law inflation, we use WKB approximation.
The scale factor during the power-law inflation is

a(\eta)=(-H_{0}\eta)^{\beta+1}.

(D.46)

where
\beta\leq 2
for power-law inflation,
\beta=-2
corresponds to
exact de Sitter and
H_{0}
denotes the characteristic energy scale associated with inflation.

Like in Sec. (
5.2
),
to solve Eq. (
5.12
), we introduce new variables such that all the time dependence can be converted into momentum
dependence
[
176
]
:

x=\ln{\frac{\beta+1}{k\eta}},\quad\mathcal{A}_{k}=e^{-\frac{x}{2}}u_{k},

(D.47)

In terms of these new variables,
\mathcal{H}
and its derivatives are given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}=e^{x}k,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}-\left(\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{2}=-\frac{1}{(\beta+1)}e^{2x}k^{2},

(D.48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{2}{(\beta+1)^{2}}e^{3x}k^{3},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\frac{a^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}{a}-4\frac{a^{\prime}a^{\prime\prime\prime}}{a^{2}}+12\left(\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{2}\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}-6\left(\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\right)^{4}=-\frac{6}{(\beta+1)^{3}}e^{4x}k^{4}.

In the redefined variables the sound speed (
5.10
) and
Z^{\prime\prime}/Z
become:

\displaystyle c_{A}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+(d_{1}-s_{1})\frac{e^{x}}{a}\frac{k}{\Lambda}+\left[(s_{1}^{2}-s_{2}-s_{1}d_{1}+d_{2})-\frac{(e_{1}-b_{1})}{(\beta+1)}e^{x}\right]\frac{e^{x}}{a^{2}}\left(\frac{k}{\Lambda}\right)^{2},

(D.49)

\displaystyle\frac{Z^{\prime\prime}}{Z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{s_{1}}{2}\left\{1+\frac{3}{\beta+1}+\frac{2}{(\beta+1)^{2}}\right\}\frac{e^{3x}}{a}\frac{k^{3}}{\Lambda}

(D.50)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{s_{1}^{2}}{4}+2s_{2}-\frac{s_{1}^{2}+5s_{2}-2b_{1}}{\beta+1}-\frac{\frac{s_{1}^{2}}{4}+2b_{1}}{(\beta+1)^{2}}-\frac{3b_{1}}{(\beta+1)^{3}}\right\}\frac{e^{4x}}{a^{2}}\frac{k^{4}}{\Lambda^{2}}.

where the scale factor
a(\eta)
in
the new variables is:

a(x)=\left(H_{0}e^{-x}\frac{\beta+1}{k}\right)^{\beta+1}.

(D.51)

Substituting Eqs. (
D.49
,
D.50
)
in Eq. (
5.12
), we have:

\frac{d^{2}u_{k}}{dx^{2}}-\left[\frac{1}{4}-e^{-2x}(\beta+1)^{2}-\left\{M_{1}e^{\beta x}+M_{3}e^{(\beta+2)x}+\left(M_{2}+M_{4}e^{2x}\right)e^{(2\beta+2)x}\frac{k^{\beta+2}}{\Lambda}\right\}\frac{k^{\beta+2}}{\Lambda}\right]u_{k}=0.

(D.52)

where,

\displaystyle M_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\beta+1)^{2}}{\left(H_{0}(\beta+1)\right)^{(\beta+1)}}(d_{1}-s_{1}),

(D.53)

\displaystyle M_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\beta+1)^{2}}{\left(H_{0}(\beta+1)\right)^{2(\beta+1)}}\left(s_{1}^{2}-s_{2}+s_{1}d_{1}+d_{2}-\frac{e_{1}-b_{1}}{\beta+1}\right),

(D.54)

\displaystyle M_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{s_{1}}{2}\frac{(\beta+1)^{2}}{\left(H_{0}(\beta+1)\right)^{(\beta+1)}}\left(1+\frac{3}{\beta+1}+\frac{2}{(\beta+1)^{2}}\right),

(D.55)

\displaystyle M_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\beta+1)^{2}}{\left(H_{0}(\beta+1)\right)^{2(\beta+1)}}\left\{\frac{s_{1}^{2}+8s_{2}}{4}-\frac{s_{1}^{2}-5s_{2}+2b_{1}}{\beta+1}-\frac{3s_{1}^{2}+4s_{2}+20b_{1}}{4(\beta+1)^{2}}-\frac{3b_{1}}{(\beta+1)^{3}}\right\}\,.

(D.56)

Eq. (
D.52
) is the equation of motion for generic power-law inflation.
It is not possible to obtain an exact analytical solution for a generic power law. Hence, in the rest of this appendix, we obtain the exact expression for the de Sitter case (
\beta=-2
) and obtain the WKB solution for an arbitrary value of
\beta
.

D.5.1
Power spectrum for de Sitter

Setting
\beta=-2
in (
D.52
), we have:

\frac{d^{2}u_{k}}{dx^{2}}+c_{A}^{2}e^{-2x}u_{k}-m_{2}^{2}u_{k}=0\,,

(D.57)

where,

\displaystyle c_{A}^{2}=1+C_{1}\frac{H_{0}}{\Lambda}+C_{2}\frac{H_{0}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle m_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}-C_{4}\frac{H_{0}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}

(D.58)

\displaystyle C_{1}=s_{1}-d_{1}\,,~~C_{4}=\frac{s_{1}^{2}}{2}-4s_{2}\,

\displaystyle C_{2}=s_{1}^{2}-s_{2}+s_{1}d_{1}+d_{2}+e_{1}-b_{1}\,.

(D.59)

As in the slow-roll case, the solution to Eq. (
D.57
) are Hankel functions
[
abramowitz+stegun
]
:

u_{k}(x)=\alpha H_{m_{2}}^{(1)}(e^{-x}m_{1})+\beta H_{m_{2}}^{(2)}(e^{-x}m_{1}),

(D.60)

Following the steps from Eq. (
5.29
)
to Eq. (
5.30
),
the gauge-field mode functions (
A_{k}
) in the super-Hubble scales reduce to:

A_{k}(\eta)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{4}}\left(\frac{-i}{\pi}\right)\Gamma(m_{2})\,\frac{c_{A}}{Z}(-\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{-c_{A}k\eta}{2}\right)^{-m_{2}},

(D.61)

where,

Z=1+d_{1}\frac{H_{0}}{\Lambda}+(d_{2}+e_{1})\frac{H_{0}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}

(D.62)

The magnetic field power spectrum is:

\mathcal{P}_{B}=\frac{H^{4}}{8\pi^{3}Z^{2}c_{A}^{3}}\Gamma^{2}(m_{2})(-c_{A}k\eta)^{4+C_{4}\frac{H_{0}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}}

(D.63)

This matches with the power-spectrum (
5.34
) for slow-roll inflation by setting
\epsilon_{1}=0
.

D.5.2
Power spectrum for generic power-law inflation

Unlike de Sitter, we can not obtain an exact analytical expression for generic power-law inflation. Instead, we use the WKB approximation to obtain the spectrum
[
176
]
. Rewriting (
D.52
) as

\frac{d^{2}{u}_{k}}{dx^{2}}+\omega^{2}(x)\,{u}_{k}(x)=0,

(D.64)

where
\omega^{2}(x)
is

\omega^{2}(x)=e^{-2x}(\beta+1)^{2}+M_{1}e^{\beta x}\frac{k^{\beta+2}}{\Lambda}+M_{2}e^{(2\beta+2)x}\frac{k^{2\beta+4}}{\Lambda^{2}}+M_{3}e^{(\beta+2)x}\frac{k^{\beta+2}}{\Lambda}+M_{4}e^{(2\beta+4)x}\frac{k^{2\beta+4}}{\Lambda^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}

(D.65)

In the sub-horizon limit (
x\rightarrow-\infty
), the third term in RHS of the above expression dominates for
\beta<-2
. Hence, in the sub-horizon region (
I
), we have:

u^{\rm I}_{k}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{I}(x)}}\,\left[A_{k}\,\exp\,i\int^{x}dy\,\omega_{I}(y)+B_{k}\,\exp\,-i\int^{x}dy\,\omega_{I}(y)\right],

(D.66)

where
{\bar{A}}(k)
and
{\bar{B}}(k)
are
k
-dependent constants that
are to be fixed by the initial conditions, and

\omega_{I}^{2}(x)=\frac{M_{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\,e^{(2\beta+2)x}\,k^{2\beta+4}\,.

(D.67)

As in the slow-roll and power-law cases, we shall assume that the gauge field is in the Bunch-Davies vacuum (
5.30
) on the sub-Hubble scales. This leads to:

\displaystyle A_{k}=-\frac{i(\beta+1)}{\sqrt{32}\,\omega_{I}(x_{i})}\,,

\displaystyle B_{k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{32}\,\omega_{I}(x_{i})}\left(i\beta+4\omega_{I}(x_{i})+i\right)\,.

(D.68)

In the super-horizon limit (
x\rightarrow\infty
), the first term in the RHS of (
D.65
) dominates for
\beta<-2
. Hence,
the super-horizon region (II), we have:

u^{\rm II}_{k}(x)=\frac{C_{k}}{\sqrt{\omega_{\rm II}(x)}}\exp{\left(\int_{x}^{x_{f}}|\omega_{\rm II}(y)|dy\right)}+\frac{D_{k}}{\sqrt{\omega_{\rm II}(x)}}\exp{\left(\int_{x}^{x_{f}}-|\omega_{\rm II}(y)|dy\right)}

(D.69)

Near the horizon crossing (
x=0
), the WKB approximation breaks down and in this region, we can approximate
\omega(x)=\alpha\,x
,
where
\alpha
depends on
M_{1},M_{2},M_{3},\Lambda
and
\beta
.
Matching the modes at the horizon exit, we have the following relation between
C_{k},D_{k}
with
A_{k},B_{k}
:

\displaystyle C_{k}=\frac{A_{k}+B_{k}}{\sqrt{8}}e^{-\Psi};~~D_{k}=\frac{B_{k}-A_{k}}{\sqrt{2}i}e^{\Psi}\,

(D.70)

where,

\Psi=\int_{x_{*}}^{x_{f}}\alpha\,y\,dy

(D.71)

Taking into account only the growing mode, the modulus squared of the mode function is:

\left|\mathcal{A}_{k}\right|^{2}=\frac{\left|B_{k}-A_{k}\right|^{2}}{2}e^{-x}\frac{e^{2\Psi}}{\sqrt{\omega(x)}}.

(D.72)

Substituting this in Eq. (
5.16
), the magnetic power spectrum at the super-horizon scales is:

\mathcal{P}_{B}(k)=\frac{k^{5}}{2\pi^{2}a^{4}}\frac{\left|B_{k}-A_{k}\right|^{2}}{2Z}e^{-x}\frac{e^{2\Psi}}{\sqrt{\omega(x)}}

(D.73)

D.6
Vector Galileon: Equation of motion in terms of Slow-roll parameters

In case of slow-roll inflation, the slow-roll parameters are defined
as
[
221
]
:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}=-\frac{H^{\prime}}{aH^{2}}=1-\frac{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{H}^{2}};~~~

\displaystyle\epsilon_{2}=\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}}{aH\epsilon_{1}}=\frac{\left(2{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}^{2}-\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}\right)}{\mathcal{H}^{2}\left(\mathcal{H}^{2}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right)}~~.

(D.74)

The equation of motion (
5.43
) for the canonical vector field
\mathbb{A}_{k}
in terms of slow-roll parameters can be obtained as

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{k}^{\prime\prime}+\left[c_{s}^{2}\,k^{2}-\frac{{\mathcal{H}}^{2}J\epsilon_{1}}{(1-J)^{2}}\,\left(1-J-3\epsilon_{1}+2J\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}(1-J)\right)\right]\,\mathbb{A}_{k}=0\,,

(D.75)

where
c_{s}
is defined in Eq. (
5.44
).
Substituting Eq.(
5.41
) in Eq. (
D.75
), the equation of motion of the physical vector field
A_{k}
is:

\displaystyle A_{k}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{2\mathcal{H}J\epsilon_{1}}{(1-J)}A_{k}^{\prime}+\frac{k^{2}(1-J+J\epsilon_{1})}{(1-J)}A_{k}=0\,.

(D.76)

Appendix E
Details on GZ effect: A plausible explanation for fast radio bursts?

In this Appendix, we provide more details on the results from chapter
6
.

E.1
Gertsenshtein-Zel
′
dovich effect and induced electromagnetic waves

Consider a background space-time with GWs
[
96
]
:

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}+h_{\alpha\beta}\,,\qquad g^{\alpha\beta}=\eta^{\alpha\beta}-h^{\alpha\beta}

(E.1)

where
h_{\alpha\beta}<<1
is the GW fluctuation, and
\eta_{\alpha\beta}=\rm{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)
is Minkowski space-time. The background space-time is generally curved; however, the results derived for Minkowski space-time carry through for the conversion factor computations. For the generic curved background, the Riemann corrections contribution is tiny
[
96
]
. Hence, we only report the results for the Minkowski space-time.

For the GWs propagating in the
z-
axis, we have
h_{xx}=-h_{yy}=h_{+}
,
h_{xy}=h_{\times}
.
Eq. (
6.1
) corresponds to a monochromatic circularly polarized GW
[
96
,
261
]
. As mentioned earlier, the presence of magnetic field transverse to the direction of propagation of GWs acts as a catalyst for the conversion process
[
260
]
. As discussed, we consider the total magnetic field in the magnetosphere to be

\displaystyle\textbf{B}(t)=\left(0,B^{(0)}_{y}+\delta B_{y}\sin(\omega_{B}t),0\right)

(E.2)

where
B^{(0)}_{y}
is static magnetic field and
B_{y}\sin(\omega_{B}t)
is the alternating (time-varying) magnetic field with frequency
\omega_{B}
and
|\delta B_{y}|<|B^{(0)}_{y}|
.
Also, we assume that the amplitude of the alternating magnetic field is two orders lower than the static magnetic field, i.e.,

|{B_{y}}/{B^{(0)}_{y}}|\approx 10^{-2}
, so that time-varying magnetic field has significant effects on the conversion (
[
268
,
267
]
). The induced electric field due to the time-varying magnetic field is [
[
305
]
]

\displaystyle\textbf{E}(z,t)=\left(-\frac{z\,\delta\omega_{B}B_{y}}{c}\cos(\omega_{B}t),0,0\right)\,.

(E.3)

In the presence of GWs, the EM field tensor is:

\displaystyle F_{\alpha\beta}=F^{(0)}_{\alpha\beta}+F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathcal{E}_{x}&\tilde{E}_{y}&\tilde{E}_{z}\\
-\mathcal{E}_{x}&0&-\tilde{B}_{z}&\mathcal{B}_{y}\\
-\tilde{E}_{y}&\tilde{B}_{z}&0&-\tilde{B}_{x}\\
-\tilde{E}_{z}&-\mathcal{B}_{y}&\tilde{B}_{x}&0\end{pmatrix},

(E.4)

where
F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is the field tensor induced due to the GWs that needs to be determined. Similarly, the induced electric

[\tilde{\textbf{E}}=\left(\tilde{E}_{x},\tilde{E}_{y},\tilde{E}_{z}\right)]

and magnetic field vectors
[\tilde{\textbf{B}}=\left(\tilde{B}_{x},\tilde{B}_{y},\tilde{B}_{z}\right)
] due to GWs are to be determined.
Note that
\mathcal{B}_{y}=B^{(0)}_{y}+\delta B_{y}\sin(\omega_{B}t)+\tilde{B}_{y}
and
\mathcal{E}_{x}=-(z\,\delta B_{y}\omega_{B}/{c})\cos(\omega_{B}t)+\tilde{E}_{x}
.
The covariant Maxwell’s equations (in the source-free region) are:

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{-g}F^{\mu\nu}\right)=0;~~\partial_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{-g}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\right)=0

(E.5)

where
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta}/2
is the dual of EM field tensor,
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}=\eta^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}/{\sqrt{-g}}
and
\eta^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}
is defined as
\eta^{0123}=1=-\eta_{0123}
is an antisymmetric tensor.
Substituting Eq. (
E.4
) in Eq. (
E.5
), and treating
F^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
and
h_{\alpha\beta}
as first-order perturbations, we have

\displaystyle{}\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}\tilde{E}_{x}-\partial_{y}\tilde{B}_{z}+\partial_{z}\tilde{B}_{y}+\left(B^{(0)}+\delta B_{y}\sin(\omega_{B}t)\right)\,\partial_{z}h_{+}

\displaystyle{}-\frac{z\,\delta B_{y}\omega_{B}}{c^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(h_{+}\,\cos(\omega_{B}t)\right)=0

(E.6a)

\displaystyle{}\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}\tilde{B}_{y}-\partial_{x}\tilde{E}_{z}+\partial_{z}\tilde{E}_{x}=0\,,

(E.6b)

where we have expressed in terms of electric and magnetic fields. Since GWs (and EM waves) propagate along the z-direction, we have
\tilde{E}_{z}=\tilde{B}_{z}=0
. Substituting Eq. (
6.1
) in the above wave equations lead to the following wave equations for
\tilde{E}_{x},\tilde{B}_{y}
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{E}_{x}}{\partial t^{2}}-\partial_{z}^{2}\tilde{E}_{x}

\displaystyle=-f_{E}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})

(E.7a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{B}_{y}}{\partial t^{2}}-\partial_{z}^{2}\tilde{B}_{y}

\displaystyle=-f_{B}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})

(E.7b)

where
f_{E/B}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})
are the forcing functions and are given by:

\displaystyle f_{E}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\frac{iA_{+}\delta B_{y}k_{g}}{2c}\left[\omega_{+}e^{i(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{+}t^{\prime})}-\omega_{-}e^{i(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{-}t^{\prime})}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{z^{\prime}A_{+}\delta B_{y}\omega_{B}}{2c^{3}}\left[\omega_{+}^{2}e^{i(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{+}t^{\prime})}+\omega_{-}^{2}e^{i(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{-}t^{\prime})}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{A_{+}B^{(0)}k_{g}\omega_{g}}{c}e^{i(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{g}t^{\prime})}

(E.8a)

\displaystyle f_{B}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\frac{iA_{+}\delta B_{y}k_{g}^{2}}{2}\left[e^{i\left(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{+}t^{\prime}\right)}-e^{i\left(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{-}t^{\prime}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{iA_{+}\delta B_{y}\omega_{B}}{2c^{2}}\left[\omega_{+}e^{i\left(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{+}t^{\prime}\right)}-\omega_{-}e^{i\left(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{-}t^{\prime}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{z^{\prime}A_{+}\delta B_{y}\omega_{B}k_{g}}{2c^{2}}\left[\omega_{+}e^{i(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{+}t^{\prime})}-\omega_{-}e^{i(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{-}t^{\prime})}\right]

\displaystyle+A_{+}B^{(0)}k_{g}^{2}e^{i\left(k_{g}z^{\prime}-\omega_{g}t^{\prime}\right)}

(E.8b)

The solutions to the wave equations (
E.7a
) and (
E.7b
) are given by:

\displaystyle F_{E/B}(z,t)=\int\int dz^{\prime}dt^{\prime}G(t,t^{\prime};z,z^{\prime})f_{E/B}(t^{\prime},z^{\prime})

(E.9)

where
F_{E/B}(z,t)
denotes the corresponding solution of the forcing function
f_{E/B}(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})
and
G(t,t^{\prime};z,z^{\prime})
is the retarded Green’s function corresponding to the wave equation and is given by:

\displaystyle G(t,t^{\prime};z,z^{\prime})=\frac{c}{2}\Theta\left(\,\,c(t-t^{\prime})-|z-z^{\prime}|\,\,\right)

(E.10)

where
\Theta-
function is non-zero only for
t\geq t^{\prime}+\frac{|z-z^{\prime}|}{c}
. Substituting the forcing functions for the electric (
E.8
) and the magnetic (
E.8
) fields, in the integral equation (
E.9
), leads to:

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{x}

\displaystyle\simeq-\frac{B^{(0)}_{y}A_{+}}{4}\,\,e^{i\left(k_{g}z-\omega_{g}t\right)}

(E.11)

\displaystyle+\frac{\delta B_{y}A_{+}\omega_{B}t}{2}\,\,e^{i\left(k_{g}z-\omega_{g}t\right)}+\frac{\delta B_{y}A_{+}}{4i}\,\left(\frac{\omega_{B}}{\omega_{g}}\right)\,\,e^{i\left(k_{g}z-\omega_{g}t\right)}

\displaystyle\tilde{B}_{y}

\displaystyle\simeq-\frac{B^{(0)}_{y}A_{+}}{4}\,\,e^{i\left(k_{g}z-\omega_{g}t\right)}

(E.12)

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta B_{y}A_{+}\omega_{g}t}{2}\,\,e^{i\left(k_{g}z-\omega_{g}t\right)}+\frac{\delta B_{y}A_{+}}{4i}\,\left(\frac{\omega_{B}}{\omega_{g}}\right)^{2}\,\,e^{i\left(k_{g}z-\omega_{g}t\right)}\,\,.

We want to mention the following points regarding the above expressions: First, in obtaining the above expressions, we have assumed that
\omega_{B}<<{\omega_{g}}
. This is valid in our case because the conversion from GWs to EM waves occurs in the
sub-GHz frequency range. This approximation leads to
\omega^{n}_{\pm}\simeq\omega^{n}_{g}\left(1\pm n\frac{\omega_{B}}{\omega_{g}}\right)
and allows us to approximate
\omega_{\pm}\approx\omega_{g}
in the exponentials. Note that we have also ignored the terms with
e^{i\omega_{g}t}
since they will lead to wave propagating along negative z-direction i.e.,
e^{i\left(k_{g}z+\omega_{g}t\right)}
, and hence are not relevant to our analysis.
Second, the last term in both expressions is tiny and can be neglected leading to Eqs. (
6.3
,
6.4
).
Lastly, GZ effect is a pure gravitational effect (due to incoming GWs) and the generation of EM waves does not require any source term (plasma or charged particles). Hence, if the incoming GWs are coherent, the emitted EM waves are coherent at resonance.

E.2
Evaluating the radius of the magnetosphere

We show that the assumption that the background magnetic field can be treated as a constant in the entire magnetosphere gives identical results to that of the background field decreasing radially.

In evaluating
\alpha_{\rm tot},
we have assumed that the background magnetic field is a constant until light cylinder radius
R_{\rm LC}
. In this section, we show that this assumption is physically consistent.

To do that, we consider the magnetic field of the pulsar magnetosphere in vacuum to be dipolar.
We evaluate the average dipolar magnetic field in the magnetosphere. In spherical polar coordinates, the dipolar magnetic field is given by
[
306
,
307
]
:

\displaystyle\left(B_{r},B_{\theta},B_{\phi}\right)=B_{*}\,\left(\frac{r_{*}}{r}\right)^{3}\,\left(2\cos\theta,\sin\theta,0\right)

(E.13)

where
B_{*}
is the magnetic field on the NS surface.

We now compute the average magnetic field on the volume between the radius of the NS (
r_{*}
) and

R_{\rm{LC}}
:

\!\!\bar{\textbf{B}}(r,\theta,\phi)=\frac{1}{V}\int_{r_{*}}^{R_{\rm{LC}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!dr\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!\!\!d\theta\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\!\!\!d\phi\,r^{2}\sin\theta\,\textbf{B}(r,\theta,\phi)

(E.14)

where
V
is the volume enclosed by both the surfaces:

\displaystyle V=\frac{4\pi}{3}\left(R_{\rm{LC}}^{3}-r_{*}^{3}\right).

(E.15)

From Eqs. (
E.14
,
E.15
), we define the integral
\mathcal{I}
as:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\mathcal{I}\equiv V\,\bar{\textbf{B}}(r,\theta,\phi)=\int_{r_{*}}^{R_{\rm{LC}}}\!\!\!\!dr\int_{0}^{\pi}\!\!\!\!d\theta\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\!\!\!d\phi\,r^{2}\sin\theta\textbf{B}(r,\theta,\phi).

(E.16)

Note that the volume average of the radial component of the magnetic field vanishes.
The non-zero contribution comes from the angular variation of the magnetic field, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bar{\textbf{B}}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{3\pi B_{*}r_{*}^{3}}{4\left(R_{\rm{LC}}^{3}-r_{*}^{3}\right)}\ln\left|\frac{R_{\rm{LC}}}{r_{*}}\right|.

(E.17)

From Eqs. (
E.16
,
E.17
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=\pi^{2}B_{*}r_{*}^{3}\ln\left|\frac{R_{\rm{LC}}}{r_{*}}\right|

(E.18)

We now compare this with the assumption that the
magnetic field is approximately constant until
R_{\rm LC}
. To do this, we substitute the
average magnetic field obtained in Eq. (
E.17
) inside the integral in Eq.(
E.16
).
Doing the radial integral between
r_{*}
to
\mathcal{R}
leads to:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=\pi^{2}B_{*}r_{*}^{3}\frac{(\mathcal{R}^{3}-r_{*}^{3})}{\left(R_{\rm{LC}}^{3}-r_{*}^{3}\right)}\,\ln\left|\frac{R_{\rm{LC}}}{r_{*}}\right|.

(E.19)

Setting
\mathcal{R}=R_{\rm{LC}}
in the above expression, we see that the two expressions (
E.18
,
E.19
) are approximately the same. Thus, even if there is a radial and angular variation in the magnetic field in the magnetosphere, we can approximate the background field to be approximately constant in the region
r_{*}\leq r\leq R_{\rm{LC}}
. Thus, our expression for the total conversion factor mimics the realistic NS regions.

E.3
Conversion factor from entire magnetosphere

In this appendix, we estimate the total conversion factor from the entire magnetosphere of the compact object.
The energy density carried by these induced EM waves is
[
305
]

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm{EM}}\simeq\frac{|A_{+}|^{2}|B^{(0)}_{y}|^{2}}{64\pi}\left[1+2\,\xi^{2}\omega_{g}^{2}\,t^{2}+2\,\xi\omega_{g}\,t\,\,\right]\,.

(E.20)

where
\xi=\delta B_{y}/B_{y}^{(0)}
. As mentioned earlier, the conversion is maximum when EM waves are approximately the same as the incoming waves.
Having obtained the energy carried by the induced EM waves, we need to obtain what fraction of wave energy is converted to EM waves
[
260
]
? To do this, we calculate the energy density carried by the GWs
[
96
]
, i. e.:

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm{GW}}=\frac{c^{2}\omega_{g}^{2}}{32\pi G}\left(|A_{+}|^{2}+|A_{\times}|^{2}\right)=\frac{c^{2}\omega_{g}^{2}}{16\pi G}\,\,|A_{+}|^{2}\,,

(E.21)

wherein second equality, we have used the fact that both the modes of GWs are generated with an equal amount of energy, i.e.,
|A_{+}|=|A_{\times}|
also referred to as isospectrality relation
[
269
]
. From Eqs. (
E.20
,
E.21
), we obtain Eq. (
6.5
).

To obtain the conversion factor in the entire magnetosphere, we define the following dimensionless parameter:

\displaystyle X=\frac{r}{R_{\rm{LC}}}

(E.22)

where
r
is the distance from the surface of neutron star to a point in the magnetosphere, i.e.,
r_{*}\lesssim\,r\lesssim\,R_{\rm{LC}}
, and
r_{*}(=10^{6}\rm{cm})
is the radius of the Neutron star (NS). In the case of Magnetar
R_{\rm LC}=10^{9}~{\rm cm}
and for NS
R_{\rm LC}=10^{7}~{\rm cm}
, hence,
X_{*}=\frac{r_{*}}{R_{\rm{LC}}}<1
. In other words, the range of
X
is
0.001\lesssim\,X\lesssim\,1
.

Since we are interested in the EM waves reaching the observer, we are interested in evaluating the conversion factor along the observer’s line of sight. Thus, we have

\displaystyle z=r=XR_{\rm{LC}}.

(E.23)

where
\theta=0
corresponds to the direction along the line-of-sight of the observer. Thus, the total conversion factor is given by the integral

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rm{tot}}=\Omega\int_{X_{*}}^{1}\alpha\,\,dX,

(E.24)

where
\Omega
is the total solid angle which is an overall constant factor because Eq.(
E.24
) is independent of the angular coordinates.

E.4
Poynting vector

In astrophysical observations of compact objects, a quantity of interest is the energy flux density which is the Poynting vector. This section evaluates the Poynting vector for magnetar and neutron star in SI unit and Jansky Hz, a widely used unit for spectral flux density in radio observations.

The Poynting vector of the induced electric field (
6.3
) and induced magnetic field (
6.4
) is
[
305
]
:

\displaystyle S_{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{8\pi}\tilde{E}_{x}\times\tilde{B}^{*}_{y}

(E.25)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{A_{+}^{2}\,|B_{y}^{(0)}|^{2}\,c}{128\pi}\left[1+2\omega_{g}\xi\frac{R_{\rm{LC}}}{c}-2\omega_{g}\omega_{B}\xi^{2}\left(\frac{R_{\rm{LC}}}{c}\right)^{2}\right]

where
\tilde{B}^{*}_{y}
is the complex conjugate of the induced magnetic field
\tilde{B}_{y}
. As mentioned above, the frequency of the alternating magnetic field is
1\rm{Hz}
for magnetar and
10^{3}\,\rm{Hz}
for the millisecond pulsar. Here again, we have assumed that
\omega_{B}<<\omega_{g}
. Rewriting the above Poynting vector in terms of
\alpha_{\rm{tot}}
, we get Eq. (
6.7
).

E.5
Current status of high-frequency GWs

Primordial black-holes, Exotic compact objects and early Universe can generate high-frequency GW (HFGW) in MHz to GHz
[
308
,
309
,
310
,
311
,
293
,
312
,
313
,
292
]
. These can generate GWs up to 14 GHz
[
293
]
. Over the last decade, many HFGW detectors are proposed, and some of them are operational. For instance, the Japanese 100 MHz detector with a
0.75~{\rm m}
armlength interferometer has been operational for a decade
[
308
,
309
]
, Holometer detector has put some limit on GWs at MHz
[
310
,
311
]
. The Bulk Acoustic GW detector experiment recently reported two MHz events after 153 days of operation
[
313
]
. A GHz GW detector is also proposed
[
293
]
. These detectors are ideally suited for searching for physics beyond the standard model (SM), like primordial black-holes, exotic compact objects and early Universe. For instance, exotic compact objects with characteristic strain
h

[
249
]
:

h\lesssim 10^{-19}C^{5/2}\left(\frac{\rm MHz}{f}\right)\left(\frac{\rm Mpc}{D}\right)

lead to the following GW amplitudes:

h_{\rm 1.4GHz,10kpc}\lesssim 10^{-21}\,,h_{\rm 1.4GHz,1Mpc}\lesssim 10^{-23}\ .

After 153 days of operation, the Bulk Acoustic GW detector experiment recently reported two MHz events
[
313
]
. According to Goryachev et al.
[
313
]
, the data corresponding to two MHz events fits best a single energy depositing event. The authors mention that the strongest observed signal results in a required characteristic strain amplitude of
h_{c}\approx 2.5\times 10^{-16}
, corresponding to a PBH merger of
m_{\mathrm{PBH}}<4\times 10^{-4}M_{\odot}
(which gives a maximum frequency at inspiral of
5.5\ \mathrm{MHz}
), at a distance of
D\approx 0.01\mathrm{pc}
. It is important to note that these are not conclusive enough. However, these detections also imply that these events are not rare.

As mentioned above, many HFGW detectors are proposed, and better estimates will be available as more and more detectors will be operational in the coming decades. This can provide better estimate of these events in the next decade.

E.6
Calculating isotropic Equivalent Luminosity and Fluence

In this appendix, we calculate the fluence and Isotropic Equivalent Luminosity (IEL). The observed fluence is defined as the product of the burst width
\Delta t=(2R_{LC})/c
and the peak flux (
S_{z}/\omega_{g}
)
[
278
,
279
]
. The energy density of the FRB electromagnetic (EM) waves
at a distance
d
from the source (NS/magnetar) in the limit
d\gg\text{source size}(r_{*})
is given by
[
280
]
:

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm EM}=\frac{L_{\rm IEL}}{4\pi d^{2}c},

(E.26)

where
L
is the isotropic equivalent luminosity.
